Performance-oriented learners interpret the same signals as threatening, activating affect regulation at the expense of strategy adaptation.
> 
> **Source:** Phase IV, Mechanism 2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Process
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #mastery-orientation #metacognitive-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "agency asymmetry" problem in context regulation?
> 
> **A:** Learners have unequal degrees of freedom to regulate context — some have private study spaces, flexible schedules, and multiple resource options; others face shared environments, constrained schedules, and limited resources. MSLQ studies measuring context regulation confound the *capacity* to regulate context with the *available opportunity* to do so. High-performing learners may appear better context regulators partly because they have more regulable contexts.
> 
> **Source:** Phase IV, Mechanism 3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Distinction
> **Tags:** #context-regulation #pintrich #limitations #equity

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "Regulatory Reflexivity Problem"?
> 
> **A:** The theoretical challenge that arises from treating motivation as a regulated domain: if the regulatory executive monitors and controls motivation, what motivates the regulatory executive? The resolution is that regulation is bootstrapped from stable dispositional tendencies (trait self-efficacy, general mastery orientation) that are themselves products of developmental history — not a fifth unregulated level, but an initially given motivational basis.
> 
> **Source:** Phase VI, Original Synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Concept
> **Tags:** #regulatory-reflexivity #original-synthesis #self-determination-theory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the tension between Pintrich's motivational regulation framework and SDT.
> 
> **A:** Pintrich treats deliberate motivational regulation (self-consequating, interest enhancement) as adaptive. SDT suggests that the highest-quality motivation (integrated regulation) develops through internalization, not deliberate management — and that controlled self-regulation may undermine autonomous motivation over time. The resolution: Pintrich's framework best describes the *transitional zone* of regulatory development, where deliberate regulation is needed; SDT describes the endpoint where autonomous motivation is fully internalized.
> 
> **Source:** Phase III, Tension; Phase VI, Synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Connection
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #motivation-regulation #pintrich

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "planfulness assumption" and why is it a limitation of Pintrich's framework?
> 
> **A:** The framework models the learner as a deliberate, rational agent who plans, monitors, controls, and reflects. This is an aspirational model — in practice, a significant proportion of learning behavior is driven by automatic, non-deliberate processes (habit, affect, implicit goal activation) that are not accessible to the kind of conscious monitoring the framework describes. The framework provides little guidance on when deliberate SRL is possible vs. when the automatic system has taken over.
> 
> **Source:** Phase V, Limitations
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Concept
> **Tags:** #planfulness-assumption #limitations #metacognitive-inaccessibility

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three abstract transfer principles extracted from Pintrich's framework?
> 
> **A:** (1) Multi-domain simultaneous regulation: effective regulation of any complex system requires monitoring across all relevant subsystems, not just the most salient. (2) Making implicit processes explicit enables improvement: processes that can be explicitly represented can be deliberately modified. (3) Environment is a first-class regulatory domain: when agent-level interventions have diminishing returns, environmental interventions are often underexplored.
> 
> **Source:** Phase VII, Abstract Principle Extraction
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Type:** Application
> **Tags:** #far-transfer #transfer-principles #multi-domain-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10
> **A:** **Q:** For what population of learners is Pintrich's framework most applicable, according to the Transitional Self-Regulation synthesis?
> 
> **A:** Learners in the middle zone of regulatory development — those who have sufficient intrinsic motivation to begin engaging but who lack the regulatory architecture to sustain engagement through difficulty, ambiguity, and extended challenge. Not novice learners with externally regulated motivation (they lack the bootstrap), and not expert learners with fully internalized motivation (they don't need deliberate regulation). This is the modal population of higher education learners.
> 
> **Source:** Phase VI, Original Synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Application
> **Tags:** #transitional-self-regulation #instructional-design #pintrich

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1
> **A:** **Q:** What are the two sub-phases of the Forethought Phase, and what does each address?
> **A:** Task Analysis (goal setting and strategic planning — the cognitive architecture of preparation) and Motivational Beliefs (self-efficacy, outcome expectations, intrinsic interest, and goal orientation — the motivational substrate that determines strategy deployment effort).
> **Source:** Phase II, Framework section
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Type:** Definition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2
> **A:** **Q:** Why is the comprehension criterion the structural counter to the fluency illusion?
> **A:** Because the comprehension criterion requires *cognitive production* (recall, explanation, example generation) to satisfy — which cannot be faked by recognition fluency. The fluency illusion operates through phenomenological ease of processing, which can feel like understanding without being it. A production-based criterion forces the learner to generate rather than recognize, revealing genuine comprehension status.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Goal Setting mechanism
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Process

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3
> **A:** **Q:** What makes an attribution "adaptive" in the SRL cycle? Give the three necessary criteria.
> **A:** An adaptive attribution is (1) SPECIFIC — names a particular mechanism or behavior rather than a global trait; (2) UNSTABLE — implies the outcome could be different with different strategy/preparation; (3) CONTROLLABLE — within the learner's influence to change. All three are necessary: a specific but uncontrollable attribution (e.g., "this is just a particularly hard domain") does not produce adaptive inference.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Causal Attribution mechanism
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4
> **A:** **Q:** What does the Calibration Engine synthesis identify as the *true output* of a well-designed Forethought-Reflection cycle over time?
> **A:** Metacognitive calibration — an increasingly accurate self-model of the learner's actual competence level (what they understand, at what depth, with what confidence). Calibration is not a skill the learner explicitly targets but an emergent structural output of consistently well-executed cycles. The engine metaphor captures this: build the cycle well, and calibration emerges as a byproduct.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Original Synthesis; Phase VI
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Connection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5
> **A:** **Q:** Why does the Self-Reflection Phase require the Forethought Phase to have produced a written comprehension criterion?
> **A:** Because self-evaluation requires a comparison between actual performance and a pre-set standard. Without a written criterion, (a) the original goal is typically lost to cognitive load interference during the reading session, and (b) self-evaluation defaults to phenomenological assessment (did this feel productive?) rather than epistemic assessment (did I achieve what I set out to understand?). Written criteria are the structural mechanism that preserves evaluability.
> **Source:** Phase IV and V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Process

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6
> **A:** **Q:** What is the three-part learning goal structure introduced in this report, and why does each component matter?
> **A:** (1) Content Anchor — specifies WHAT concept/mechanism is targeted (activates attentional direction); (2) Comprehension Criterion — specifies WHAT LEVEL of understanding is targeted and how it will be verified (provides evaluable standard for Self-Reflection); (3) Transfer Target — specifies WHY this understanding is wanted (provides functional relevance that sustains intrinsic motivation). Remove any component and a specific mechanism is compromised.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Goal Setting section
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Definition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "expertise trap" in relation to goal orientation, and why does it specifically affect advanced learners?
> **A:** The expertise trap is the pattern where intellectually sophisticated learners who have succeeded academically have internalized a performance orientation — their identity is linked to appearing competent. When encountering genuinely difficult material in private learning, this manifests as avoidance of the most challenging sections, surface processing, and resistance to explicit self-evaluation (which makes deficits visible and thus threatening). Advanced learners are *more* susceptible, not less, because they have more identity investment in appearing competent.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Goal Orientation section
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Application

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Handoff Mechanism, and why is it structurally necessary rather than merely helpful?
> **A:** The Handoff Mechanism is a physical link (a specific field in the Self-Reflection template that feeds directly into the next session's Forethought template) that transfers the adaptive inference from one cycle to the next. It is structurally necessary because adaptive inferences — the most valuable output of the Self-Reflection Phase — evaporate without explicit capture and forward connection. In a solo learning context without social accountability structures, the handoff mechanism is the only reliable mechanism for actually applying inferences across sessions rather than generating and discarding them.
> **Source:** Phase V, Application 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Type:** Application

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Self-Reflection Phase *construct* self-efficacy for the next Forethought Phase?
> **A:** Self-efficacy in a solo PKB learning system is built primarily through mastery experiences — direct experience of successfully achieving comprehension criteria. These experiences are *generated* in the Performance Phase but *interpreted* in the Self-Reflection Phase through the attribution mechanism. If Self-Reflection produces accurate mastery-referenced self-evaluation and controllable attributions, self-efficacy updates accurately. If it produces ability-based attributions, self-efficacy either deflates (failure → I'm not capable) or becomes inflated through fluency illusion (apparent success without genuine comprehension). Self-efficacy is therefore a *dynamic output* of Self-Reflection, not a stable input to Forethought.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Return-and-Deepen: Self-Efficacy Revisited
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Connection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10
> **A:** **Q:** What does the SDT research add to the language design requirements for SRL tools that Achievement Goal Theory does not cover?
> **A:** SDT's autonomy support research adds that *controlling language* — even self-directed controlling language without any external agent — undermines intrinsic motivation. "I must understand this" generates internal pressure that is phenomenologically equivalent to external control and activates the same amotivation mechanisms. Achievement Goal Theory covers the mastery/performance orientation distinction but does not address the autonomy dimension — the difference between "I want to understand X because it connects to [valued goal]" (autonomy-supportive) and "I must understand X" (controlling). Both are mastery-framed; only one is autonomy-supportive.
> **Source:** Phase V, Language Architecture section
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Type:** Distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are Weiner's three dimensions of causal attribution, and what motivational outcome does each primarily determine?
> **A:** (1) Locus of causality (internal/external) → determines pride/shame; (2) Stability (stable/unstable) → determines expectancy for future outcomes; (3) Controllability (controllable/uncontrollable) → determines guilt vs. shame and others' judgments.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #attribution-theory #weiner #motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between self-efficacy and self-esteem, and why does this distinction matter for inner speech?
> **A:** Self-efficacy is a domain-specific, task-specific, future-oriented *capability judgment* ("can I do this?"). Self-esteem is a global evaluation of self-worth. The distinction matters because efficacy-undermining inner speech targets specific tasks and domains, making it more amenable to reframing, while self-esteem threats are more global and harder to address through domain-specific vocabulary.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-efficacy #bandura #self-esteem #inner-speech

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the attribution-efficacy cascade step-by-step, from the trigger event (comprehension difficulty) to the comprehension outcome.
> **A:** (1) Comprehension difficulty triggers automatic attribution search; (2) inner speech vocabulary determines the attribution made; (3) attribution is processed on stability and controllability dimensions; (4) stable/uncontrollable attribution reduces efficacy; unstable/controllable maintains it; (5) reduced efficacy reduces effort and depth of processing; (6) comprehension outcome feeds back into the next attribution cycle.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #attribution #self-efficacy #inner-speech #mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is introjected regulation in SDT, what is its characteristic inner speech vocabulary, and why is it problematic despite being internal?
> **A:** Introjected regulation is motivation driven by internally administered approval/shame contingencies; characterized by *should, must, have to, ought to* inner speech constructions. It is problematic despite being internal because it produces controlled (non-autonomous) motivation, generating pressure and conditional self-worth rather than genuine endorsement — with the same cognitive and motivational costs as external control (reduced deep processing, lower well-being, reduced intrinsic motivation).
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #sdt #introjection #regulatory-language #autonomous-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** How do mastery-approach and performance-avoidance goal orientations differentially interpret the same comprehension difficulty during reading?
> **A:** Mastery-approach: difficulty is information about what remains to be learned ("what do I need to understand here?"). Performance-avoidance: difficulty is potential evidence of inadequate competence ("if I don't understand this, it means I'm not smart enough") — triggers withdrawal or surface processing to minimize the shame risk.
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #mastery-goal #performance-avoidance #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** Using the four-domain vocabulary architecture, reframe the inner speech statement "I'm just not good at this level of abstraction" across all four domains.
> **A:** (1) Attribution: "I haven't yet developed the schema for this type of abstract argument"; (2) Efficacy: "I can develop this skill by working through examples systematically"; (3) Regulatory: "I'm choosing to develop this capacity because it matters for my PKB work"; (4) Goal: "My goal is to understand the specific mechanism by which [X abstraction] works, well enough to paraphrase it."
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #vocabulary-architecture #reframing #inner-speech #practical

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** What is the temporal grammar difference between fixed mindset and growth mindset inner speech, and how does it connect to Weiner's stability dimension?
> **A:** Fixed mindset inner speech is atemporally stable — "I can't" implies "I never will" because ability is treated as static. Growth mindset inner speech is temporally open — "I can't *yet*" grammatically marks the current limitation as time-limited. This maps directly onto Weiner's stability dimension: growth mindset reframes ability as unstable (developable) rather than stable (fixed), which preserves expectancy for future success.
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #growth-mindset #attribution-theory #temporal-grammar #stability

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the verbal persuasion source of self-efficacy, and why does self-directed inner speech function through the same mechanism?
> **A:** Verbal persuasion is one of Bandura's four sources of self-efficacy: evaluative statements from credible others that influence capability beliefs. Self-directed inner speech functions through the same mechanism because the individual is simultaneously the source and the recipient of efficacy-relevant evaluations; the individual's credibility with themselves (based on their performance history) makes inner speech a potent efficacy input — both positive ("I've handled this kind of material before") and negative ("I can never follow this level of argument").
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-efficacy #verbal-persuasion #bandura #inner-speech

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the self-reflection phase vocabulary audit, and why is conducting it after each reading session motivationally important?
> **A:** The vocabulary audit reviews: (1) dominant attribution type (ability vs. strategy); (2) regulatory register (controlled vs. autonomous); (3) comprehension criterion applied; (4) efficacy accuracy (evidence-based vs. emotion-contaminated). It is important because the self-reflection phase's attributions feed directly into the forethought phase of the next learning episode — maladaptive attributions made during self-reflection become the prior motivational conditions for future reading.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #srl #self-reflection #zimmerman #inner-speech

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** Explain how goal framing vocabulary affects comprehension monitoring criteria — specifically how mastery-approach vs. performance-avoidance goal language changes what "counts" as successful comprehension.
> **A:** Goal vocabulary establishes the criterion against which monitoring is calibrated. Mastery-approach goal language ("I want to understand the mechanism") sets a demanding criterion (genuine mechanistic understanding); performance-avoidance language ("I need to get through this") sets a surface criterion (completing the reading). When the criterion is surface, genuine comprehension failures go undetected and corrective re-reading is never triggered — producing the appearance of having read without the substance of comprehension.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #comprehension-monitoring #achievement-goals #metacognition #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** Write a Level 3 (Process Goal) in the hierarchical goal construction template for reading a dense theoretical section of a Foundational Report.
> **A:** "I will read actively, paraphrasing each paragraph in my own words before moving on. I will treat failure to paraphrase as a comprehension monitoring signal requiring re-reading or prior knowledge activation rather than a cue to continue. If I lose the thread of the argument, I will explicitly reconstruct the argument structure before proceeding."
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #goal-setting #hierarchical-goals #srl #forethought-phase

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the predicative compression problem in inner speech, and why does it create vocabulary risk during academic reading?
> **A:** Vygotsky's inner speech is condensed and predicative — only the dynamic predicate is retained, not the full propositional subject. During reading, a complex comprehension failure may compress to a single predicate like "can't" or "lost," which bundles together radically different attribution types (ability failure, strategy failure, prior knowledge gap). The risk is that compressed predicates automatically activate ability attributions (the most cognitively economical inference) rather than the more accurate, more controllable strategy or knowledge attributions — initiating the vicious attribution-efficacy spiral.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #inner-speech #vygotsky #attribution #predicative-compression

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four mediating mechanisms through which goals enhance performance according to Locke and Latham's goal-setting theory?
> **A:** Direction (focusing attention on goal-relevant activities), effort (difficult goals mobilise greater effort), persistence (goals sustain effort over time), and strategy development (goals promote the search for task-relevant strategies when existing approaches prove insufficient).
> **Source:** Section 1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #goal-setting-theory #locke-latham #mechanisms

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the critical difference between autonomous motivation and controlled motivation in SDT?
> **A:** Autonomous motivation involves volitional engagement — acting from self-endorsed interest, values, or identity. Controlled motivation involves compliance — acting from external pressure (external regulation) or internalised guilt/self-worth contingencies (introjection). Both can produce behaviour, but autonomous motivation produces deeper processing, greater persistence, and enhanced well-being.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #sdt #autonomous-motivation #controlled-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** In the 2×2 achievement goal framework, what distinguishes mastery-approach from performance-approach orientation?
> **A:** Mastery-approach defines success as developing competence and deepening understanding; performance-approach defines success as demonstrating competence relative to others. Mastery-approach is associated with deep processing and intrinsic interest; performance-approach is associated with surface processing but sometimes positive performance outcomes.
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #mastery-goal #performance-goal

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three phases of Zimmerman's self-regulated learning model and what occurs in each?
> **A:** Forethought (goal-setting, strategic planning, self-motivation beliefs), Performance (self-control strategies, self-observation/monitoring), Self-reflection (self-evaluation against standards, causal attribution, adaptive/defensive inferences).
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #srl #zimmerman #learning-phases

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** How does the overconfidence effect threaten self-assessment in PKM, and what mechanisms can improve calibration?
> **A:** The overconfidence effect causes learners to systematically overestimate their understanding, especially after superficial exposure. Calibration-improving mechanisms include: generation-based testing (explaining without notes), application-based assessment (using concepts in novel situations), connection-based evaluation (linking to other concepts), and temporal spacing (reassessing after delay).
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition #calibration #self-assessment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** What is the specificity-autonomy dialectic and why is it the central design tension in PKM goal-setting?
> **A:** Goal-setting theory shows that specific goals enhance performance (through variance reduction and attention focusing), while SDT shows that autonomy enhances motivational quality. These pull in opposite directions: high specificity can undermine autonomy by constraining choice, while high autonomy can sacrifice the direction-giving benefits of specificity. The resolution is to be specific about *what understanding looks like* while preserving autonomy over *how that understanding is pursued*.
> **Source:** Section 2, 6, 8
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #specificity-autonomy #goal-design #motivational-grammar

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the motivational grammar framework integrate the requirements of the four theoretical frameworks?
> **A:** Template: [Understanding verb] + [domain specification] + [capability criterion] + [autonomy marker] + [temporal integration]. Understanding verb → mastery orientation (AGT). Domain specification → specificity (goal-setting theory). Capability criterion → calibration improvement (metacognition). Autonomy marker → need satisfaction (SDT). Temporal integration → planning support (SRL).
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #motivational-grammar #goal-design #integration

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes formative assessment from summative assessment, and why is formative assessment the appropriate model for PKM?
> **A:** Formative assessment is designed to inform and improve ongoing learning (assessment *for* learning); summative assessment certifies or ranks attainment (assessment *of* learning). Formative assessment's three functions — making criteria transparent, providing evidence of current standing, and indicating gap-closing actions — align with PKM's purpose of continuous knowledge development rather than terminal certification.
> **Source:** Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #formative-assessment #pkm-design #self-assessment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four dimensions of linguistic microstructure in PKM goals that this report identifies as causally operative?
> **A:** (1) Verb choice — understanding verbs vs. production verbs activates mastery vs. performance orientation. (2) Object specification — understanding-focused vs. output-focused objects. (3) Autonomy markers — language preserving choice over method. (4) Temporal framing — integrated timeframes vs. controlling deadlines.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #goal-language #linguistic-framing #motivational-grammar

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does introjection function as a motivational hazard in self-directed PKM practice?
> **A:** Introjection is partially internalised regulation driven by guilt, anxiety, or self-worth contingency rather than genuine valuing. In PKM, it manifests when self-imposed assessment triggers self-punishment ("I *should* have finished this") rather than informational processing ("What does this difficulty tell me about my strategy?"). Assessment structures must be designed to function informationally (supporting competence) rather than controllingly (triggering introjection).
> **Source:** Section 2, 7
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #introjection #sdt #self-assessment #pkm-hazards

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition | Intention-Action Gap
> **A:** **Front**: What is the "intention-action gap" and what does Sheeran's meta-analysis reveal about the strength of intentions as predictors of behavior?
> **Back**: The intention-action gap is the systematic failure of sincerely formed intentions to generate corresponding action. Sheeran's meta-analysis found that intentions account for only ~28% of behavioral variance — a modest relationship, with the gap most severe for novel, complex behaviors requiring sustained effort. The gap is primarily architectural (absence of specific volitional structures) rather than motivational.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction | Goal Specificity vs. Goal Commitment
> **A:** **Front**: Distinguish goal specificity from goal commitment. Which is a necessary condition for the feedback loop, and which is the primary moderator of the difficulty-performance relationship?
> **Back**: **Goal specificity** is the precision of the performance standard — necessary for the feedback-standard comparison loop that drives regulatory adjustment. **Goal commitment** is the determination to pursue the goal despite obstacles — the primary moderator of the difficulty-performance relationship. Both are required: specificity without commitment produces clear targets without follow-through; commitment to a vague goal cannot close the monitoring loop.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Process | Zimmerman's SRL Phases
> **A:** **Front**: Describe the three phases of Zimmerman's Cyclical SRL Model and identify the single most consequential process in each phase for long-term learning development.
> **Back**: (1) **Forethought**: Goal-setting and strategic planning most consequential — determines the quality of goal architecture feeding all downstream monitoring. (2) **Performance**: Self-observation/monitoring most consequential — generates the feedback against which performance can be evaluated. (3) **Self-Reflection**: Causal attribution most consequential — determines whether performance information generates adaptive or maladaptive inferences for the next cycle, making the model genuinely recursive or merely repetitive.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition | Implementation Intention
> **A:** **Front**: What is an implementation intention? What is its mechanism, and what does it accomplish that goal intentions alone cannot?
> **Back**: An implementation intention is an "if-then" plan: "If [situational cue], then [goal-directed response]." Mechanism: creates a prospective memory link that automatizes cue-initiated behavior, bypassing voluntary recall. What it accomplishes: reduces volitional demand at initiation by converting a deliberate decision into an automatic cue-triggered response. This addresses the prospective memory failure problem — the failure to *remember* to act when the opportunity arises — while leaving goal commitment unchanged.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application | Action Orientation vs. State Orientation
> **A:** **Front**: What is Kuhl's distinction between action orientation and state orientation, and what is the regulatory consequence of each under conditions of difficulty and failure?
> **Back**: **Action orientation**: attention directed toward task-relevant problem-solving strategies; cognitive resources remain goal-directed under difficulty. **State orientation**: attention directed toward internal emotional states (rumination, anxiety); cognitive resources consumed by affective processing rather than problem-solving. Under failure: action-oriented learners process negative affect and return to goal-directed cognition; state-oriented learners remain in affective preoccupation, functionally blocking continued goal-directed behavior.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Distinction | Mastery vs. Performance Goal Orientation (Adaptive Regulation)
> **A:** **Front**: How do mastery and performance goal orientations produce different regulatory responses to failure, and why?
> **Back**: **Mastery**: failure interpreted as strategic information — signals which approach is not working, activates strategy search and effort intensification. Failure is informative rather than identity-threatening, so regulatory response is approach rather than avoidance. **Performance**: failure interpreted as evidence of lower ability or standing relative to others — activates defensive processing, self-protective withdrawal, and disengagement. Because performance goals define success comparatively, failure carries identity-threatening content that mastery-framed failure does not.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition | Metacognitive Calibration
> **A:** **Front**: What is metacognitive calibration and why is it important for SRL? What is the core limitation of endogenous calibration in novice learners?
> **Back**: Metacognitive calibration is the accuracy of the correspondence between self-assessed understanding and actual understanding. Crucial for SRL because inaccurate monitoring generates maladaptive regulatory decisions — specifically, prematurely moving on from inadequately processed material. Core limitation: novices lack the domain standards needed to evaluate their own knowledge quality (Dunning-Kruger), creating systematically overconfident monitoring. Solution: externally-sourced calibration (retrieval practice, error analysis, expert feedback) provides accurate performance signals the endogenous system cannot.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Connection | SDT and Achievement Goal Theory
> **A:** **Front**: What is the relationship between Self-Determination Theory's autonomous/controlled distinction and Achievement Goal Theory's mastery/performance distinction? Are they independent?
> **Back**: Partially overlapping but distinguishable. Mastery-approach goals are more likely to be autonomously regulated; performance-avoidance goals more likely controlled. However, the dimensions are not identical: a performance-approach goal can be autonomously adopted (genuinely desiring to achieve high comparative standing) and a mastery goal can be controlled (experiencing mastery pursuit as obligatory). SDT's distinction concerns the *source* of motivation (autonomous vs. controlled); achievement goal theory concerns the *definition of success* (competence development vs. comparative standing). Both independently predict engagement quality and outcomes.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Process | The Self-Concordance Mechanism
> **A:** **Front**: What is the self-concordance model, what does it predict, and what is the mechanism that explains why self-concordant goals produce better long-term outcomes?
> **Back**: Self-concordance (Sheldon & Elliot): degree to which pursued goals align with genuine personal values rather than external pressure. Predicts greater sustained effort, better obstacle-weathering, higher attainment probability, and greater wellbeing upon attainment. Mechanism: self-concordant goal pursuit satisfies basic psychological needs (competence, autonomy, relatedness), which sustain intrinsic motivation and continued investment. Non-self-concordant goals, even when attained, fail to generate need satisfaction because the pursuit was not genuinely one's own — producing the "arrival fallacy" of hollow achievement.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition | Volitional Control
> **A:** **Front**: What is volitional control and why does it function as the "neglected middle" of learning psychology?
> **Back**: Volitional control: the capacity to translate committed intentions into sustained action, protecting goal pursuit against competing motivations, environmental distractions, emotional disturbances, and cognitive temptations. "Neglected middle": it occupies the space between motivation (studied extensively in the predecisional phase) and metacognition (studied during task execution), but the specific mechanisms by which commitment is sustained *across* learning episodes were historically under-theorized. Volition is distinct from motivation (which initiates) and metacognition (which monitors); it is the sustaining capacity that maintains goal-directed behavior when motivation fluctuates.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Application | Building a Goal Architecture
> **A:** **Front**: List the five components of an effective goal architecture based on the integrated framework in this report.
> **Back**: 1. **Decomposition**: Long-horizon aspirations broken into specific proximal milestones with clear success criteria; 2. **Challenge calibration**: Goals set at the upper edge of credible attainability (productive struggle range); 3. **Feedback structure**: Explicit mechanisms for generating real-time performance-to-standard comparison data; 4. **Implementation intentions**: "If-then" behavioral plans for each significant learning activity; 5. **Motivational check**: Self-concordance verification — explicit connection to genuine personal values rather than external compliance.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Connection | Goal-Setting Theory Mechanisms → SRL Phase Mapping
> **A:** **Front**: Map Locke and Latham's four mediating mechanisms (direction, effort, persistence, strategy activation) onto Zimmerman's three SRL phases.
> **Back**: **Forethought**: *Direction* (goal specifies which activities warrant attention and effort); *Strategy activation* (specific goal triggers planning of the relevant strategies); *Persistence-scaffolding* (commitment formation crosses the Rubicon, establishing the volitional foundation for sustained effort). **Performance**: *Effort* mobilization (discrepancy-monitoring drives energy expenditure proportional to gap and goal commitment); *Persistence* (action orientation and volitional control mechanisms sustain effort despite difficulty). **Self-Reflection**: *Strategy activation continued* (failure attribution that targets strategy rather than ability triggers adaptive strategy search for the next cycle).

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Definition | Self-Regulated Learning
> **A:** **Front**: Define self-regulated learning (SRL) in terms of what it requires across cognitive, volitional, and motivational dimensions.
> **Back**: **Self-Regulated Learning**: the degree to which learners are metacognitively, motivationally, and behaviorally active participants in their own learning (Zimmerman). **Cognitively**: learners monitor comprehension, plan strategically, and evaluate strategies adaptively. **Volitionally**: learners protect goal pursuit against competing demands, implement intentions systematically, and manage emotional states that would otherwise disrupt engagement. **Motivationally**: learners pursue learning with mastery orientation, autonomous regulation, and high task-specific self-efficacy. SRL is the integrated operation of all three dimensions; impairment in any single dimension degrades the whole system.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the distinction between textbase and situation model in Kintsch's reading comprehension model?
> **A:** The textbase is a coherent propositional representation of *what the text says* — the literal semantic content of the text's sentences. The situation model is a mental representation of *what the text describes* — the state of affairs in the world or domain the text refers to. Genuine comprehension requires situation model construction; textbase representation alone supports recognition but not transfer or integration.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #comprehension #reading-theory #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and why is it motivationally significant?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the metacognitive error of mistaking the ease of text processing (reading fluency) for depth of comprehension (situation model construction). It is motivationally significant because it prevents deployment of deeper processing strategies (if the reader believes they understand, they stop working to understand), produces false assessment confidence, and when assessment performance fails, this failure is misattributed to ability rather than monitoring — reinforcing entity theories and performance-avoidance goals.
> **Source:** Sections 2 & 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition #fluency-illusion #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three basic psychological needs in SDT, and how does each apply specifically to academic reading?
> **A:** (1) **Autonomy** — the need to experience reading as self-initiated and volitionally chosen. In reading: supporting autonomy means providing genuine rationales, meaningful choice within required content, and framing reading for personal understanding rather than external compliance. (2) **Competence** — the need to experience efficacy and mastery. In reading: satisfied by successful comprehension experiences, progressive difficulty calibration, and strategy training that produces genuine understanding. (3) **Relatedness** — the need for connection with others. In reading: satisfied by shared reading practices, discussion communities, and meaningful connection of reading content to valued relationships and communities.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #SDT #basic-psychological-needs #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance goals in the context of academic reading.
> **A:** **Mastery-approach:** Reading to develop genuine understanding; predicts deep processing, persistence, positive affect. **Mastery-avoidance:** Reading to avoid failure-to-understand, driven by comprehension anxiety; predicts self-monitoring and persistence from fear. **Performance-approach:** Reading to demonstrate superior comprehension relative to others; predicts adequate assessed performance but surface strategic reading. **Performance-avoidance:** Reading to avoid appearing incompetent; predicts avoidance, surface processing, learned helplessness — most consistently negative outcome profile.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #goal-orientation #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the logic of the three-tier strategy hierarchy and what does it mean to "start at the wrong tier"?
> **A:** The hierarchy sequences strategies according to prerequisite logic: Tier 1 (cognitive foundation) creates conditions Tier 2 presupposes; Tier 2 (comprehension strategies) creates conditions Tier 3 presupposes. Starting at the wrong tier means applying strategies that require conditions not yet in place. Example: teaching elaborative interrogation (Tier 2) to a reader with profound vocabulary gaps (Tier 1 deficit) will fail because the reader cannot generate meaningful elaborations when basic comprehension is blocked by unknown vocabulary. The diagnostic must precede the prescription.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #strategy-hierarchy #reading-motivation #intervention-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is self-explanation as a reading strategy and why is it more effective than summarization?
> **A:** Self-explanation involves generating explanatory verbalizations or written articulations of what a passage means in the reader's own words, how it relates to prior content and knowledge, and what gaps remain. It is more effective than summarization because it requires construction of causal relationships and inferential connections that the text may not state explicitly — forcing situation model construction rather than accurate textbase restatement. Summarization can be achieved at the textbase level; genuine self-explanation requires situation model access.
> **Source:** Section 6.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #self-explanation #comprehension-strategy #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model and what are the three phases applied to academic reading?
> **A:** **Forethought phase:** Before reading — goal setting (what do I want to genuinely understand?), strategic planning (which strategies are appropriate for this text?), motivational self-management (intrinsic goal activation, prior knowledge preparation). **Performance phase:** During reading — strategy deployment, comprehension monitoring, fix-up strategy selection and execution when failures are detected. **Self-reflection phase:** After reading — self-evaluation of comprehension quality, causal attribution of successes and failures, adaptive planning for the next reading session. The cycle makes self-regulated reading self-improving rather than repetitively consistent.
> **Source:** Section 7.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning #metacognition #reading-self-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is interest theory's four-phase model and what distinguishes situational from individual interest?
> **A:** Four phases: (1) Triggered situational interest — externally triggered by environmental features; (2) Maintained situational interest — sustained by engaging content value; (3) Emerging individual interest — beginning of stored value and re-engagement tendency; (4) Well-developed individual interest — stable, self-sustaining predisposition to engage. **Situational interest** is triggered by specific contextual features (an engaging text, a surprising demonstration) and dissipates when those features are absent. **Individual interest** is a relatively stable personal predisposition that motivates seeking out domain-relevant content across contexts, independent of specific environmental triggers.
> **Source:** Section 3.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #interest-theory #situational-interest #individual-interest #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Why is retrieval practice more effective than re-reading for long-term retention, and how should it be applied during academic reading?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is more effective because the act of retrieving strengthens the memory trace through effortful reconstruction (a desirable difficulty that enhances encoding), whereas re-reading produces recognition familiarity (high processing fluency) without effortful retrieval — it improves performance on immediate tests but not long-term retention. **Application during reading:** After completing a section, close or cover the text and write from memory the main points and key arguments. Check accuracy against the text. The gaps identified during retrieval attempts are precisely the content requiring focused re-reading or further elaboration.
> **Source:** Sections 6.3 and 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #retrieval-practice #spaced-repetition #desirable-difficulties #reading-strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Motivational-Cognitive Reading Stack and what diagnostic implication does it carry?
> **A:** The Stack integrates four systems that must each meet threshold development for motivated academic reading to be self-sustaining: **Layer 1** (Cognitive Pre-Conditions: fluency, vocabulary, working memory management, prior knowledge), **Layer 2** (Comprehension-Generating Strategies: self-explanation, elaborative interrogation, retrieval practice), **Layer 3** (Motivation Architecture: autonomous regulation, mastery goals, intrinsic goal framing, individual interest). **Diagnostic implication:** Identify the lowest layer where the system is failing. A reader who cannot sustain motivation despite good comprehension skills needs Layer 3 work. A reader who is motivated but comprehends shallowly needs Layer 2 strategy training. A reader whose strategies fail needs Layer 1 development. Starting intervention at the correct layer is essential.
> **Source:** Section 6.4 original synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #motivational-cognitive-stack #original-synthesis #reading-motivation #diagnostic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Motivational Deployment Problem, and how does it differ from strategy ignorance or strategic incapacity?
> 
> **Answer:** The Motivational Deployment Problem is the structural gap between knowing effective learning strategies work and consistently deploying them. It differs from strategy ignorance (not knowing what works) and strategic incapacity (lacking the skill) in that the learner has both knowledge and capacity but fails to deploy due to motivational barriers — primarily the aversive phenomenological experience of the strategies that work best.
> 
> **Source:** Section 1: The Motivation-Strategy Nexus
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #motivation #learning-science #strategy-deployment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** How does autonomous motivation differ from controlled motivation in its effect on the SRL monitoring phase?
> 
> **Answer:** Autonomous motivation supports honest monitoring: comprehension failure is experienced as information, not threat, enabling accurate self-assessment. Controlled motivation produces ego-defensive monitoring: learners subjectively upgrade comprehension estimates under introjected regulation (where self-esteem depends on performance), filtering out monitoring signals that would damage self-concept. Same monitoring capacity; opposite willingness to attend to what it reveals.
> 
> **Source:** Section 5.2: Where SDT Intersects the SRL Cycle
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #self-regulated-learning #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Process):** Walk through the hierarchical order of the seven learning effects, explaining why the ordering reflects both cognitive depth and motivational cost.
> 
> **Answer:** Generation (pause, produce) → Testing Effect (retrieve, tolerate failure) → Spacing (tolerate forgetting intervals) → Interleaving (tolerate productive confusion) → Elaboration (sustain effortful connection-making) → Self-Explanation (confront comprehension gaps) → Metacognitive Strategy (regulate all others honestly). Each requires progressively more: more working memory demand, more tolerance of aversive experience, more SRL phase engagement, and more motivational infrastructure. The hierarchical ordering means lower strategies are prerequisite motivational entry points for higher strategies.
> 
> **Source:** Section 3: The Seven Learning Strategies
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #learning-effects #hierarchy #desirable-difficulties

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the desirable difficulties paradox, and why does it create a persistent motivational problem?
> 
> **Answer:** The desirable difficulties paradox is the finding that the conditions which feel most like productive learning (massed practice, re-reading, blocked practice) are among the least effective for long-term retention, while conditions that feel like ineffective or failed learning (spaced practice, retrieval failure, interleaving confusion) are the most effective. The persistent motivational problem is this: learners use subjective sense of learning as their primary feedback signal for strategy effectiveness, but this signal is systematically inverted for the strategies that matter most.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.4: Cognitive Load and the Learning Process
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #metacognitive-distortion #strategy-selection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Application):** You have just completed reading a complex 5000-word report on cognitive science. Design the optimal post-reading protocol using specific learning effects to maximize long-term retention.
> 
> **Answer:** (1) **Free recall test** (testing effect at maximum): Close the report and write everything you can recall, without aids, for 15 minutes. (2) **Gap review** (generation + self-explanation): Open the report only to check against your recall; for each gap, generate a self-explanation of why you missed it and create a permanent note. (3) **Connection mapping** (elaboration): Open your vault and create bidirectional links from the new material to at least 3-5 existing permanent notes, writing 1-2 sentences explaining each connection. (4) **SRS card creation** (spacing + retrieval practice for future): Create Anki cards from the key concepts, ensuring question forms require generation rather than recognition. (5) **Set spaced review intervals**: Schedule a retrieval test at 1 day, 3 days, 1 week, 2 weeks, 1 month from reading.
> 
> **Source:** Section 7.2: Strategy Deployment in the Reading-to-PKB Workflow
> **Difficulty:** Application
> **Tags:** #reading-practice #pkb-workflow #seven-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Connection):** What is the relationship between the basic psychological needs (SDT) and mastery goal adoption (Achievement Goal Theory)?
> 
> **Answer:** Need satisfaction and mastery goals are bidirectionally reinforcing. Autonomy need satisfaction predicts mastery goal adoption: learners who experience their study as self-chosen and personally meaningful define success in terms of developing genuine understanding rather than social performance. Competence need satisfaction supports mastery goals by calibrating challenge to the growing edge, keeping failure informative rather than devastating. Conversely, mastery goals support need satisfaction: orienting toward competence development creates the contexts in which genuine improvement (competence need) and self-chosen challenge (autonomy need) are most likely to be experienced. Performance goals tend to frustrate both needs by making success contingent on social comparison rather than genuine development.
> 
> **Source:** Section 4.2: Achievement Goal Theory
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #achievement-goal-theory #motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Fluency Illusion, and what is its primary consequence for self-regulated reading?
> 
> **Answer:** The fluency illusion is the systematic tendency to mistake the cognitive ease of re-reading (increased familiarity = easier processing = faster reading) for a reliable signal of learning depth. The primary consequence for self-regulated reading is monitoring corruption: the monitoring phase of the SRL cycle receives false positive signals (high comprehension estimates) from re-reading, preventing the control phase from triggering the strategy adjustments (retrieval practice, self-explanation) that would actually produce durable encoding. Learners who rely heavily on re-reading are operating with calibrated monitoring for the wrong signal.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.5: Prior Knowledge and the Fluency Illusion
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion #metacognitive-monitoring #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** Distinguish between the motivational costs of the testing effect, the spacing effect, and the interleaving effect. Why is each difficult for a different reason?
> 
> **Answer:** **Testing effect**: Motivationally costly because it provides immediate, undeniable evidence of forgetting and incompetence — it directly confronts the self-efficacy of learners with performance goals. **Spacing effect**: Motivationally costly because it requires deliberately *tolerating* forgetting between sessions — the subjective experience of apparently losing progress between reviews feels like evidence the strategy is failing, when it is actually the mechanism of its success. **Interleaving effect**: Motivationally costly because interleaved practice consistently produces *lower* subjective confidence and *lower* apparent performance than blocked practice during the session itself, even though it produces better long-term transfer. The interleaving problem is the starkest inversion of the performance-mastery feel: you feel less competent during the strategy that actually makes you most competent.
> 
> **Source:** Sections 3.2, 3.3, 3.4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #testing-effect #spacing-effect #interleaving

















































































## Open Questions

> [!open-question] **Does Motivational Regulation Transfer Across Domains?** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> Is the capacity to regulate motivation a general, transferable skill — or a set of domain-specific competencies? If general, then motivational regulation training in one context should generalize to others. If domain-specific, then the MSLQ's subscales and Wolters' strategy taxonomy describe families of similar but domain-specific behaviors. The answer has significant implications for how motivational regulation should be taught. Current evidence is insufficient to resolve this question definitively.

> [!open-question] **How Long Does Attribution Retraining Take?** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> The attribution retraining literature (Wilson & Linville, 1985; Walton & Wilson, 2018) suggests that explicit attribution training can shift patterns in relatively brief interventions. However, these studies typically measure effects over weeks, not months, and use structured social interventions. For a solo adult learner implementing attribution retraining through self-directed SRL tools, the timeline and durability of change are empirically unexplored. This is among the most important open questions for PKB learners developing their SRL practice.

> [!open-question] **What Is the Minimum Viable Forethought Phase?** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> The analysis in this report describes a comprehensive Forethought Phase architecture. But cognitive load constraints are real: a full forethought protocol takes time and effort that competes with the reading session itself. What is the minimum set of Forethought Phase elements that preserves the essential structural benefits — attentional direction, effort calibration, mastery orientation activation — without excessive overhead? This question is practically crucial for sustainable implementation.

> [!open-question] **What is the Natural Ecological Validity of Inner Speech Vocabulary Research?** *(from [[attribution-self-efficacy-inner-speech-vocabulary-foundational-report-2026-03-25]])*
> **The Question:** Nearly all research on inner speech in academic contexts uses verbal protocols (think-aloud) or retrospective self-report, which may not capture the condensed, predicative character of natural inner speech. Is the vocabulary that appears in verbal protocols representative of the vocabulary that actually operates during reading?
> 
> **What We Know:** Fernyhough and colleagues have documented that inner speech in naturalistic settings (experience sampling methodology) often takes non-verbal or partially verbal forms — visual imagery, emotional tone, fragmentary language — rather than full propositions. The rich attribution and efficacy language in this report may correspond more to *potential* inner speech (what could be verbalized if prompted) than to *occurring* inner speech.
> 
> **What We Don't Know:** Whether natural inner speech during academic reading is predominantly verbal (and thus vocabulary-modifiable), whether the non-verbal components carry the same motivational content as their verbal counterparts, and whether deliberate verbal practice generalizes to condensed inner speech.
> 
> **Research Direction:** Experience-sampling studies of inner speech during authentic academic reading, combined with concurrent comprehension measures, would provide important data.
> 
> **See also:** [[inner-speech]], [[metacognitive-monitoring]], [[silent-reading-fluency]]

> [!open-question] **Can Self-Assessment in PKM Achieve Adequate Calibration?** *(from [[goal-setting-and-assessment-language-in-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> The overconfidence effect is one of the most robust findings in metacognition research. Can a self-directed learner, without external feedback, develop sufficiently calibrated self-assessment? The generation-testing and capability-based criteria proposed in this report are theoretically motivated interventions, but their effectiveness in sustained PKM practice has not been empirically validated. This remains the most important unanswered question for PKM assessment design.

> [!open-question] **Open Question 1: Metacognitive Calibration Development Mechanisms** *(from [[goal-setting-and-self-regulated-learning-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> We know that metacognitive calibration improves with domain expertise, and that external calibration devices (retrieval practice, error analysis) accelerate this development. What we do not fully understand is the specific mechanism by which externally sourced calibration produces endogenous monitoring improvement — does accurate external feedback train the monitoring system to apply similar accuracy criteria to future introspective judgments, or does it simply substitute for impaired introspection without improving it? The answer has direct implications for interventions: are we improving the metacognitive system itself or constructing prostheses for it? See: [[metacognition]], [[metacognitive-monitoring]]

> [!open-question] **Open Question: Long-Term Maintenance of Academic Reading Motivation in Autodidacts** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> The research base on reading motivation is disproportionately derived from K-12 and college-aged study populations. The long-term developmental trajectory of intrinsic reading motivation in adult autodidacts who engage in structured self-directed academic reading programs is almost completely unstudied. Questions that remain open: How do autonomous regulation and individual interest interact across multi-decade reading programs? What are the characteristic motivational patterns and crises in long-term autodidact intellectual development? How does the relationship between intrinsic motivation and reading habit change across the reading development trajectory?

> [!open-question] **The Reframe Stability Problem** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> The Motivational Framing Hierarchy proposed in this report assumes that cognitive reframes ("failed recall is the learning event") can be durably internalized and that internalized reframes produce sustained motivational change. But how stable are cognitive reframes under conditions of high stress, academic pressure, or chronic fatigue? The psychological literature on cognitive reappraisal (Gross & John) suggests that some individuals are substantially more capable of successful cognitive reappraisal than others, and that reappraisal capacity is itself a trait with a developmental trajectory. The report has not addressed individual differences in cognitive reappraisal capacity or the conditions under which motivational reframes are most likely to succeed vs. fail.









## Protocols & Methods

> [!protocol] **Four-Area Self-Assessment Protocol (PKB Learning Sessions)** *(from [[pintrichs-framework-srl-focused-analysis-2026-03-23]])*
> **Context:** For use at the beginning and end of any significant PKB learning session. Takes 3–5 minutes and implements Pintrich's monitoring framework in miniature.
> 
> **Steps:**
> 1. **Before:** Record current self-efficacy for the session's learning goals (1-10) and interest/value level (1-10). Identify potential environmental threats (distractions, resource gaps). Set cognitive, motivational, and behavioral goals explicitly.
> 2. **During:** At natural pause points, check: Am I understanding? Is my motivation holding? Is my effort consistent? Is my environment still supportive? Apply regulation where monitoring signals a problem.
> 3. **After:** Note what worked and what failed in each of the four areas. Attribute specifically (what caused the failures — strategy, environment, motivation, behavior?). Record one adjustment for the next session.
> 
> **Success Criteria:** The protocol is working when your post-session attribution analysis identifies different causes across different areas — suggesting that area-specific monitoring is genuinely happening rather than undifferentiated "it went well/badly."
> 
> **Common Pitfalls:** Conflating cognitive monitoring ("I understood this material") with motivational monitoring ("I wanted to keep going"). Attributing all failures to effort (a single-area attribution) rather than investigating which of the four areas was the actual locus of breakdown.

> [!protocol] **Pre-Reading Forethought Protocol v1.0** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **STEP 1: MICRO-SURVEY (2 min)**
> Read the title, abstract/introduction summary, major headings, and conclusion callouts of the target text.
> 
> *Prompt:* "Based on my micro-survey, this text's central analytical claim or question appears to be: _______________"
> 
> *Prompt:* "The sections/phases most important for my current learning priorities are: _______________"
> 
> ---
> 
> **STEP 2: PRIOR KNOWLEDGE ACTIVATION (1 min)**
> Without looking at the text, write what you already know about the central topic.
> 
> *Prompt:* "What I already understand about [topic]: _______________"
> 
> *Prompt:* "What I'm uncertain about or what questions I have going into this: _______________"
> 
> ---
> 
> **STEP 3: GOAL SETTING — THREE-PART STRUCTURE (2 min)**
> Set your learning goal using the three-part structure:
> 
> *Content Anchor:* "The specific concept, mechanism, or argument I want to understand from this reading: _______________"
> 
> *Comprehension Criterion:* "I will know I've achieved this when I can (without looking at the text): _______________"
> [Examples: explain the mechanism in my own words / give 2 original examples / identify the implications for X / connect this to [existing PKB note] by articulating how they relate]
> 
> *Transfer Target:* "I want this understanding because: _______________"
> [Examples: it connects to / extends / resolves a question in [note] / it informs how I approach [practice] / it answers the question I had after [prior session]]
> 
> ---
> 
> **STEP 4: STRATEGIC PLANNING (1–2 min)**
> Select your reading strategy based on your schema level:
> 
> *Schema Level Check:* Is this topic schema-rich (I have significant prior knowledge) or schema-sparse (genuinely new territory)?
> 
> *If schema-rich:* Focus strategies: Elaborative interrogation during reading ("Why does this work this way?"), connection-noting to existing PKB nodes, identify tensions with prior understanding.
> 
> *If schema-sparse:* Focus strategies: Structural reading first (central claim + main support), tolerance for ambiguity in details, flag-for-follow-up rather than pressure for immediate comprehension, modest comprehension criterion.
> 
> *My strategy for this session:* _______________
> 
> ---
> 
> **STEP 5: SELF-EFFICACY CALIBRATION (1 min)**
> *Prompt:* "My confidence that I can meet the comprehension criterion I set, given the strategy I've chosen, is: ___/10"
> 
> *Calibration check:* "What specifically would need to be true for me to achieve this comprehension criterion? [Check if your confidence rating reflects the actual task demands]"
> 
> *If confidence is below 5:* Consider whether the comprehension criterion needs to be adjusted for this session (is it too ambitious for the current schema level?), or whether a preliminary reading/schema-building activity is needed first.
> 
> ---
> 
> **HANDOFF FIELD FROM PREVIOUS SESSION:**
> *Adjustments from prior Self-Reflection:* [Copy from last session's Reflection Note] _______________

> [!protocol] **Post-Reading Self-Reflection Protocol v1.0** *(from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24 (1)]])*
> **STEP 1: SELF-EVALUATION — TESTING AGAINST THE CRITERION (3–4 min)**
> 
> *Retrieval task:* Without looking at the text or your notes, attempt to satisfy the comprehension criterion you set in Forethought:
> - Write your explanation of the target mechanism/claim in your own words.
> - Generate the examples, connections, or implications you targeted.
> 
> *Evaluation:* Compare your retrieval output against your comprehension criterion.
> 
> *Prompt:* "Against my comprehension criterion, my actual comprehension level is: [Full / Substantial / Partial / Minimal]"
> 
> *Prompt:* "The specific aspects I understood well: _______________"
> 
> *Prompt:* "The specific aspects I did not fully understand: _______________"
> 
> ---
> 
> **STEP 2: CAUSAL ATTRIBUTION — STRATEGY SEARCH (3–4 min)**
> 
> [IMPORTANT: Before generating any global conclusion about your ability or the text's difficulty, complete steps 2a and 2b.]
> 
> *Step 2a — Strategy-Level Explanation Search:*
> 
> "For each aspect I did not understand fully, identify a specific strategy-level explanation:"
> 
> | What I didn't understand | Possible strategy-level explanation |
> |--------------------------|-------------------------------------|
> | ________________         | Did I have sufficient prior schema? Y/N |
> |                          | Was my goal specific enough? Y/N |
> |                          | Did I use elaborative interrogation? Y/N |
> |                          | Did I self-explain at section boundaries? Y/N |
> |                          | Was my comprehension criterion calibrated to my actual level? Y/N |
> 
> *Step 2b — Attribution Classification:*
> 
> "The most accurate attribution for the gap between my comprehension criterion and my actual comprehension is: _______________"
> 
> [This attribution should be: SPECIFIC (names a mechanism), UNSTABLE (could be different with different strategy), CONTROLLABLE (within your influence)]
> 
> *If your natural attribution is "this material is too difficult for me" or "I'm not good at this type of content":* These are global, stable, internal attributions. Before accepting them, list at least two specific strategy-level explanations you could test in the next session. If you cannot generate two, the current session's strategy was probably not sufficiently deliberate to generate diagnostic data.
> 
> ---
> 
> **STEP 3: SELF-REACTION — AFFECT AND ADAPTIVE INFERENCE (2–3 min)**
> 
> *Affect acknowledgment:* "My honest emotional reaction to this session's outcomes: _______________"
> 
> [Note: Frustration and disappointment are appropriate and informative. Shame or global self-criticism are signals that the attribution step may not be working as intended — return to 2a.]
> 
> *Adaptive Inference:* "Based on my attribution, the specific strategy adjustment that would most improve my next Forethought Phase is: _______________"
> 
> [This must be specific enough to enter directly into the next session's Forethought template. Examples:
> - "Activate prior schema by writing a recall summary before reading"
> - "Reduce comprehension criterion scope to two mechanisms rather than the whole report"
> - "Add elaborative interrogation at each major section break"
> - "Read a lighter introduction to [topic X] before the next report on this domain"]
> 
> ---
> 
> **STEP 4: HANDOFF TO NEXT FORETHOUGHT (1–2 min)**
> 
> "Adjustments for next Forethought Phase session: _______________"
> 
> [Copy this field to the HANDOFF FIELD of your next session's Forethought Protocol]
>
