## References

- **Zimmerman, B.J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P.R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The primary theoretical source for the three-phase cyclical model analyzed throughout this report. Essential reading for anyone implementing the Forethought/Self-Reflection framework. Particularly valuable for its detailed explication of the two-subsystem architecture of each phase.
- **Bandura, A. (1997). *Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control*. Freeman.**: The foundational treatment of self-efficacy theory. The four sources of self-efficacy discussed in Part II and the mastery experience architecture underlying the motivational design in Part VIII are grounded in this work. Chapters 3 and 8 are most directly applicable.
- **Weiner, B. (1985). An attributional theory of achievement motivation and emotion. *Psychological Review*, 92(4), 548–573.**: The foundational empirical paper for the three-dimensional attribution analysis developed in Part IV. Essential for the Zone 3 attribution protocol in Template 2. The locus-stability-controllability framework is articulated here in its most rigorous form.
- **Ames, C. (1992). Classrooms: Goals, structures, and student motivation. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 84(3), 261–271.**: The defining paper for the mastery/performance goal orientation distinction as applied to educational settings. Grounds the language bank in Template 4 and the motivational architecture in Part VIII. Directly applicable to the design of self-directed learning environments.
- **Dweck, C.S. (1986). Motivational processes affecting learning. *American Psychologist*, 41(10), 1040–1048.**: Dweck's foundational articulation of entity vs. incremental theories of intelligence and their relationship to achievement goal orientations. The implicit theory → attribution → self-reaction chain described in Part IV is developed most clearly here.
- **Chi, M.T.H., de Leeuw, N., Chiu, M.H., & LaVancher, C. (1994). Eliciting self-explanations improves understanding. *Cognitive Science*, 18(3), 439–477.**: The definitive empirical demonstration of the self-explanation effect that grounds the Cold Reconstruction protocol and the Self-Explanation Scaffold in Template 6. The mechanism — self-explanation forces integration of new information with prior knowledge — is directly applicable to PKB-integrated reading.
- **Bjork, R.A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing About Knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: The foundational treatment of desirable difficulties and the fluency illusion. Essential context for understanding why the Cold Reconstruction protocol's difficulty is a feature rather than a bug, and why defensive inferences toward easier reading conditions are typically miscalibrated.
- **Black, P., & Wiliam, D. (1998). Assessment and classroom learning. *Assessment in Education*, 5(1), 7–74.**: The landmark meta-analysis on formative assessment. While focused on classroom contexts, the principles directly apply to self-directed learning: specific behavioral feedback (not global evaluations), process-focused criteria, and the critical role of clear learning intentions established before instruction. The PKB Forethought protocol's goal-setting design reflects these principles.
- **Flavell, J.H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The paper that established metacognition as a major research field. The declarative/procedural/conditional distinction developed in Part VII derives from this framework. Essential for understanding the metacognitive substrate of both Forethought and Self-Reflection.
- **Ericsson, K.A., Krampe, R.T., & Tesch-Römer, C. (1993). The role of deliberate practice in the acquisition of expert performance. *Psychological Review*, 100(3), 363–406.**: The foundational deliberate practice paper. The SRL cycle's feedback loop — as the mechanism by which self-directed reading develops toward expertise — structurally parallels Ericsson's deliberate practice architecture. The connection between these two frameworks is one of the most productive cross-domain links in the PKB.

*Citations sourced from [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]]*

---

- **Zimmerman, B.J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P.R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The primary theoretical source for the three-phase cyclical model analyzed throughout this report. Essential reading for anyone implementing the Forethought/Self-Reflection framework. Particularly valuable for its detailed explication of the two-subsystem architecture of each phase.

- **Bandura, A. (1997). *Self-Efficacy: The Exercise of Control*. Freeman.**: The foundational treatment of self-efficacy theory. The four sources of self-efficacy discussed in Part II and the mastery experience architecture underlying the motivational design in Part VIII are grounded in this work. Chapters 3 and 8 are most directly applicable.

- **Weiner, B. (1985). An attributional theory of achievement motivation and emotion. *Psychological Review*, 92(4), 548–573.**: The foundational empirical paper for the three-dimensional attribution analysis developed in Part IV. Essential for the Zone 3 attribution protocol in Template 2. The locus-stability-controllability framework is articulated here in its most rigorous form.

- **Ames, C. (1992). Classrooms: Goals, structures, and student motivation. *Journal of Educational Psychology*, 84(3), 261–271.**: The defining paper for the mastery/performance goal orientation distinction as applied to educational settings. Grounds the language bank in Template 4 and the motivational architecture in Part VIII. Directly applicable to the design of self-directed learning environments.

- **Dweck, C.S. (1986). Motivational processes affecting learning. *American Psychologist*, 41(10), 1040–1048.**: Dweck's foundational articulation of entity vs. incremental theories of intelligence and their relationship to achievement goal orientations. The implicit theory → attribution → self-reaction chain described in Part IV is developed most clearly here.

- **Chi, M.T.H., de Leeuw, N., Chiu, M.H., & LaVancher, C. (1994). Eliciting self-explanations improves understanding. *Cognitive Science*, 18(3), 439–477.**: The definitive empirical demonstration of the self-explanation effect that grounds the Cold Reconstruction protocol and the Self-Explanation Scaffold in Template 6. The mechanism — self-explanation forces integration of new information with prior knowledge — is directly applicable to PKB-integrated reading.

- **Bjork, R.A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing About Knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: The foundational treatment of desirable difficulties and the fluency illusion. Essential context for understanding why the Cold Reconstruction protocol's difficulty is a feature rather than a bug, and why defensive inferences toward easier reading conditions are typically miscalibrated.

- **Black, P., & Wiliam, D. (1998). Assessment and classroom learning. *Assessment in Education*, 5(1), 7–74.**: The landmark meta-analysis on formative assessment. While focused on classroom contexts, the principles directly apply to self-directed learning: specific behavioral feedback (not global evaluations), process-focused criteria, and the critical role of clear learning intentions established before instruction. The PKB Forethought protocol's goal-setting design reflects these principles.

- **Flavell, J.H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The paper that established metacognition as a major research field. The declarative/procedural/conditional distinction developed in Part VII derives from this framework. Essential for understanding the metacognitive substrate of both Forethought and Self-Reflection.

- **Ericsson, K.A., Krampe, R.T., & Tesch-Römer, C. (1993). The role of deliberate practice in the acquisition of expert performance. *Psychological Review*, 100(3), 363–406.**: The foundational deliberate practice paper. The SRL cycle's feedback loop — as the mechanism by which self-directed reading develops toward expertise — structurally parallels Ericsson's deliberate practice architecture. The connection between these two frameworks is one of the most productive cross-domain links in the PKB.

- **Pintrich, P.R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P.R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451–502). Academic Press.**: The central theoretical statement of Pintrich's framework. Establishes the four-area architecture and articulates the claim that motivation and context are regulated domains. Essential reading for understanding the theoretical commitments analyzed in Phases II and IV. Particularly valuable for its extended discussion of goal orientation's interaction with cognitive strategy use.

- **Pintrich, P.R. (2004). A conceptual framework for assessing motivation and self-regulated learning in college students. *Educational Psychology Review, 16*(4), 385–407.**: The most concise and complete statement of the 4×4 matrix framework. The 2004 paper is the standard reference for the framework's architecture. Supports Phases II and III directly. Recommended sections: the matrix table (pp. 390–392) and the discussion of context regulation (pp. 397–400).

- **Pintrich, P.R., & De Groot, E.V. (1990). Motivational and self-regulated learning components of classroom academic performance. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 82*(1), 33–40.**: The foundational empirical study establishing that metacognitive self-regulation and cognitive strategy use predict academic performance, while self-efficacy and intrinsic goal orientation predict cognitive strategy use. The basis for the mediation claim discussed in Phase III. One of the most-cited papers in educational psychology.

- **Wolters, C.A. (2003). Regulation of motivation: Evaluating an underemphasized aspect of self-regulated learning. *Educational Psychologist, 38*(4), 189–205.**: The primary empirical and theoretical treatment of motivational regulation within Pintrich's framework. Develops the taxonomy of motivational regulation strategies and presents survey evidence for their effects on academic outcomes. Directly supports Phase III's treatment of the motivational regulation evidence base.

- **Pintrich, P.R., Smith, D.A.F., Garcia, T., & McKeachie, W.J. (1991). *A Manual for the Use of the Motivated Strategies for Learning Questionnaire (MSLQ)*. University of Michigan.**: The technical manual for the most widely used SRL measurement instrument. Essential for understanding how the framework's theoretical architecture has been operationalized. Particularly valuable for its discussion of subscale construction and validation.

- **Zimmerman, B.J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P.R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: Zimmerman's definitive statement of his cyclical model, published in the same volume as Pintrich's 2000 paper. The juxtaposition clarifies the theoretical differences between the two approaches. Supports the comparative analysis throughout this report.

- **Deci, E.L., & Ryan, R.M. (2000). The "what" and "why" of goal pursuits: Human needs and the self-determination of behavior. *Psychological Inquiry, 11*(4), 227–268.**: The central SDT paper against which the tension with Pintrich's motivational regulation framework is analyzed. Essential for understanding why high-quality autonomous motivation may not require deliberate motivational regulation. Supports Phase III's tension analysis and Phase VI's synthesis.

- **Winne, P.H., & Hadwin, A.F. (1998). Studying as self-regulated learning. In D.J. Hacker, J. Dunlosky, & A.C. Graesser (Eds.), *Metacognition in educational theory and practice* (pp. 277–304). Lawrence Erlbaum.**: [[Winne-&-Hadwin's-Metacognitive-Processing-Model|Winne and Hadwin's]] information processing model of SRL provides a useful comparison case — more cognitively detailed than Pintrich, less motivationally comprehensive. The comparison clarifies why Pintrich's motivational area is a theoretical advance over purely cognitive SRL models.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The foundational chapter establishing the three-phase cyclical SRL model with the detailed sub-components of each phase. The primary theoretical source for the Forethought and Self-Reflection Phase architectures analyzed in this report. Essential reading for Phases II and IV.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a self-regulated learner: An overview. *Theory into Practice, 41*(2), 64–70.**: The most accessible summary of Zimmerman's model, translating the theoretical framework into practical implications for educational contexts. Supports Phases II, V, and the appendix tools.

- **Bandura, A. (1997). *Self-efficacy: The exercise of control*. W. H. Freeman.**: The definitive statement of self-efficacy theory, including the four-source model and the calibration/accuracy distinction that is central to the mechanism analysis in Phase IV. Recommended for the self-efficacy sections of Phase IV specifically.

- **Weiner, B. (1985). An attributional theory of achievement motivation and emotion. *Psychological Review, 92*(4), 548–573.**: The foundational paper establishing the three-dimensional attribution framework (locus, stability, controllability) and its motivational consequences. Central to Phase IV's attribution mechanism analysis and the Self-Reflection Phase tool design.

- **Dweck, C. S. (1986). Motivational processes affecting learning. *American Psychologist, 41*(10), 1040–1048.**: The paper establishing the mastery/helpless response pattern distinction and its connection to goal orientation and implicit theories of intelligence. Central to the adaptive/defensive inference distinction and the language architecture principles in Phase V.

- **Ames, C. (1992). Classrooms: Goals, structures, and student motivation. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 84*(3), 261–271.**: Establishes the mastery-referenced evaluation standard as the key variable producing adaptive motivational orientations. Supports the self-evaluation mechanism analysis in Phase IV and the language architecture in Phase V.

- **Hattie, J., & Timperley, H. (2007). The power of feedback. *Review of Educational Research, 77*(1), 81–112.**: Meta-analytic synthesis of feedback research establishing the three levels of effective formative feedback (task, process, self-regulation). Directly supports the language architecture principles in Phase V. Recommended for the practical tools section.

- **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a practically useful theory of goal setting and task motivation: A 35-year odyssey. *American Psychologist, 57*(9), 705–717.**: The definitive review of goal-setting theory research establishing specificity, difficulty, and proximity as the key goal features. Supports the goal-setting mechanism analysis in Phase IV and the three-part learning goal framework.

- **Black, P., & Wiliam, D. (1998). Assessment and classroom learning. *Assessment in Education, 5*(1), 7–74.**: Foundational review establishing formative assessment practices as among the most powerful interventions in educational research. Supports the language architecture principles in Phase V and the self-evaluation component of the Self-Reflection Phase design.

- **Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (2000). The "what" and "why" of goal pursuits: Human needs and the self-determination of behavior. *Psychological Inquiry, 11*(4), 227–268.**: The theoretical architecture of SDT, including the autonomy-support principles underlying the motivational language framework in Phase V. Essential for understanding why controlling self-talk is motivationally hazardous.

- **Schunk, D. H., & Swartz, C. W. (1993). Goals and progress feedback: Effects on self-efficacy and writing achievement. *Contemporary Educational Psychology, 18*(3), 337–354.**: Empirical research demonstrating the specific effects of goal specificity and progress feedback on self-efficacy and achievement in academic writing contexts. Supports Phase III's evidence review of goal-setting effects.

- **Walton, G. M., & Wilson, T. D. (2018). Wise interventions: Psychological remedies for social and personal problems. *Psychological Review, 125*(5), 617–655.**: The "wise interventions" framework providing an evidence-based account of how brief, targeted attributional and mindset interventions produce durable behavioral change. Supports the attribution retraining dimension of Self-Reflection Phase tool design.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (1989). A social cognitive view of self-regulated academic learning. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 81*(3), 329–339.**: The foundational paper establishing the SRL model within Bandura's Social Cognitive Theory framework. Zimmerman articulates the three phases and their interrelations for the first time. Supports Phases II and IV throughout.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The definitive chapter-length treatment of the three-phase cyclical model. Provides the most complete account of Self-Reflection Phase components (self-judgment and self-reaction). Foundational for the entire analysis.

- **Bandura, A. (1977). Self-efficacy: Toward a unifying theory of behavioral change. *Psychological Review, 84*(2), 191–215.**: The foundational self-efficacy paper. Establishes the four sources of efficacy information and the prospective, domain-specific nature of self-efficacy beliefs. Supports the self-efficacy feedback loop analysis in Phase IV.

- **Weiner, B. (1985). An attributional theory of achievement motivation and emotion. *Psychological Review, 92*(4), 548–573.**: Weiner's comprehensive attributional framework, establishing the locus-controllability dimensions. The theoretical foundation for the Attributional Bridge construct. Supports Phase III and Phase IV.

- **Dweck, C. S., & Goetz, T. E. (1978). Attributions and learned helplessness. In J. Harvey, W. Ickes, & R. Kidd (Eds.), *New directions in attribution research* (Vol. 2). Erlbaum.**: Establishes the attribution-learned helplessness link and demonstrates the asymmetric development of maladaptive attribution patterns. Evidence for the asymmetric ratchet construct in Phase VI.

- **Pajares, F., & Miller, M. D. (1994). Role of self-efficacy and self-concept beliefs in mathematical problem solving: A path analysis. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 86*(2), 193–203.**: Path analysis demonstrating that self-efficacy mediates the performance-outcome to subsequent performance link through attribution. Direct evidence for the attribution-mediation finding in Phase III.

- **Butler, R. (1987). Task-involving and ego-involving properties of evaluation: Effects of different feedback conditions on motivational perceptions, interest, and performance. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 79*(4), 474–482.**: Demonstrates that mastery-standard evaluation produces better self-efficacy, engagement, and learning outcomes than normative evaluation. Supports Phase III analysis of evaluative standards.

- **Elliot, A. J., & Dweck, C. S. (1988). Goals: An approach to motivation and achievement. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 54*(1), 5–12.**: Establishes the goal orientation moderation of attributional tendencies — mastery goal orientation predicts effort/strategy attribution, performance orientation predicts ability attribution. Supports Phase III.

- **Paris, S. G., & Winograd, P. (1990). How metacognition can promote academic learning and instruction. In B. J. Jones & L. Idol (Eds.), *Dimensions of thinking and cognitive instruction* (pp. 15–51). Erlbaum.**: Articulates the three types of metacognitive knowledge (declarative, procedural, conditional) and their developmental patterns. Foundation for the conditional knowledge-attribution quality link in Phase IV.

- **Pekrun, R. (2006). The control-value theory of achievement emotions: Assumptions, corollaries, and implications for educational research and practice. *Educational Psychology Review, 18*(4), 315–341.**: Establishes that academic emotions are determined by perceived control and task value, and that they influence cognitive processing including attributional tendencies. Supports the emotional mediation limitation noted in Phase V.

- **Crocker, J., & Park, L. E. (2004). The costly pursuit of self-esteem. *Psychological Bulletin, 130*(3), 392–414.**: Provides the theoretical foundation for understanding defensive inference as psychologically functional in contexts where performance is used as a signal of self-worth. Supports the tension-identified callout in Phase IV.

- **Weiner, B. (1985). An attributional theory of achievement motivation and emotion. *Psychological Review, 92*(4), 548–573.**: Weiner's seminal theoretical paper presenting the three-dimensional (locus, stability, controllability) framework as an integrated theory with explicit emotional and behavioral predictions. This is the primary theoretical source for Phase II's attribution framework and the attribution vocabulary architecture in Phase V. Recommended reading: the sections on affect-attribution linkages and the controllability-guilt/shame relationship.

- **Bandura, A. (1997). *Self-efficacy: The exercise of control*. Freeman.**: Bandura's comprehensive synthesis of three decades of self-efficacy research; Chapter 3 (Sources of Self-Efficacy) is directly relevant to the verbal persuasion mechanism discussed in this report. Chapter 8 covers self-regulatory efficacy and academic functioning. Essential for anyone deepening beyond this report's treatment of efficacy.

- **Fernyhough, C., & Alderson-Day, B. (2015). Inner speech: Development, cognitive functions, phenomenology, and neurobiology. *Psychological Bulletin, 142*(9), 931–965.**: Comprehensive review covering the development, structure, measurement, and neural basis of inner speech; the most current authoritative synthesis. Directly relevant to Phase II's treatment of inner speech structure and the limitations discussion's methodological concerns. Highly recommended for the expansion topic on inner speech measurement.

- **Deci, E. L., Koestner, R., & Ryan, R. M. (1999). A meta-analytic review of experiments examining the effects of extrinsic rewards on intrinsic motivation. *Psychological Bulletin, 125*(6), 627–668.**: Meta-analysis (96 studies) demonstrating the robust undermining effect of controlling extrinsic rewards on intrinsic motivation; provides empirical foundation for the SDT-based claim that controlled regulatory language undermines engagement quality. Read alongside the more recent Vansteenkiste et al. work on autonomous vs. controlled goal framing.

- **Elliot, A. J., & McGregor, H. A. (2001). A 2×2 achievement goal framework. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 80*(3), 501–519.**: Introduces the 2×2 goal framework (mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, performance-avoidance) and its differential motivational consequences; provides the empirical grounding for Phase III's treatment of goal orientation and its inner speech vocabulary correlates.

- **Perry, R. P., Hechter, F. J., Menec, V. H., & Weinberg, L. E. (1993). Enhancing achievement motivation and performance in college students: An attributional retraining perspective. *Research in Higher Education, 34*(6), 687–723.**: Field experiment demonstrating that structured attribution retraining (teaching students to attribute failure to controllable causes) significantly improves academic performance and persistence in college students; key empirical foundation for the claim that vocabulary reframing produces genuine motivational change rather than merely relabeling.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Self-efficacy: An essential motive to learn. *Contemporary Educational Psychology, 25*(1), 82–91.**: Zimmerman's synthesis of self-efficacy within the SRL framework; directly relevant to the Forethought Phase treatment and the connection between efficacy beliefs and goal setting that underpins Phase V's protocols.

- **Dweck, C. S. (1999). *Self-theories: Their role in motivation, personality, and development*. Psychology Press.**: Dweck's theoretical synthesis connecting implicit theories of intelligence (fixed/growth mindset) to goal orientation, attribution patterns, and academic behavior; provides the intellectual foundation for the temporal grammar of inner speech discussed in Phase III.

- **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (1990). *A Theory of Goal Setting and Task Performance*. Prentice-Hall.**: The foundational monograph of goal-setting theory. Establishes the four mechanisms (direction, effort, persistence, strategy) and the specificity-difficulty relationship central to this report. Chapter 3 on moderators and Chapter 7 on self-set goals are most relevant.

- **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a practically useful theory of goal setting and task motivation: A 35-year odyssey. *American Psychologist*, 57(9), 705–717.**: The definitive review summarising 35 years of empirical evidence for goal-setting theory. Includes the most complete statement of the four mediating mechanisms and the boundary conditions for goal effects.

- **Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (1985). *Intrinsic Motivation and Self-Determination in Human Behavior*. Plenum.**: The founding text of self-determination theory. Chapters on cognitive evaluation theory and organismic integration theory are essential background for understanding how goals interact with autonomy, competence, and relatedness needs.

- **Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L. (2000). Self-determination theory and the facilitation of intrinsic motivation, social development, and well-being. *American Psychologist*, 55(1), 68–78.**: The most widely cited overview of SDT, presenting the autonomy-competence-relatedness framework and the internalisation continuum in accessible form. Section on autonomy-supportive environments is directly relevant to PKM design.

- **Elliot, A. J., & McGregor, H. A. (2001). A 2 × 2 achievement goal framework. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 80(3), 501–519.**: The paper introducing the four-cell achievement goal framework that resolves earlier binary models. The mastery-approach cell is the most theoretically relevant to PKM, but the mastery-avoidance cell has underappreciated implications.

- **Mueller, C. M., & Dweck, C. S. (1998). Praise for intelligence can undermine children's motivation and performance. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 74(1), 33–52.**: The landmark study demonstrating that the *linguistic framing* of feedback (process vs. person praise) produces dramatically different motivational trajectories. Foundational evidence for this report's claim that goal language is causally operative.

- **Vansteenkiste, M., Simons, J., Lens, W., Sheldon, K. M., & Deci, E. L. (2004). Motivating learning, performance, and persistence: The synergistic effects of intrinsic goal contents and autonomy-supportive contexts. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 87(2), 246–260.**: Demonstrates the interaction between goal contents (intrinsic vs. extrinsic) and context (autonomy-supportive vs. controlling), showing that intrinsic goals in autonomy-supportive contexts produce the strongest learning outcomes.

- **Black, P., & Wiliam, D. (1998). Inside the black box: Raising standards through classroom assessment. *Phi Delta Kappan*, 80(2), 139–148.**: The seminal review establishing that formative assessment produces substantial learning gains. Its framework of transparency, evidence, and gap-closing actions translates directly to PKM assessment-layer design.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The definitive statement of Zimmerman's tripartite SRL model. Essential for understanding how forethought, performance, and self-reflection phases interact and how goal-setting functions as a forethought activity.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451–502). Academic Press.**: Integrates achievement goal theory with self-regulated learning theory. The 4×4 framework (phases × areas) introduced here is essential for understanding the full scope of regulatory activity in PKM.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. In G. Bower (Ed.), *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation* (Vol. 26, pp. 125–173). Academic Press.**: The foundational paper on the monitoring-control framework of metacognition. The meta-level/object-level architecture is the basis for understanding how self-assessment operates in learning contexts.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The paper that named metacognition as a field and proposed the person-task-strategy taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge. Foundational vocabulary for all subsequent metacognitive research.

- **Foundational: Goal-Setting Theory**: **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P.** (1990). *A theory of goal setting and task performance*. Prentice-Hall.
**Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P.** (2002). Building a practically useful theory of goal setting and task motivation. *American Psychologist, 57*(9), 705–717. https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.57.9.705
**Latham, G. P.** (2012). *Work motivation: History, theory, research, and practice* (2nd ed.). Sage.

- **Foundational: Cyclical SRL Model and Development**: **Zimmerman, B. J.** (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.
**Zimmerman, B. J., & Martinez-Pons, M.** (1990). Student differences in self-regulated learning. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 82*(1), 51–59.
**Zimmerman, B. J., & Bandura, A.** (1994). Impact of self-regulatory influences on writing course attainment. *American Educational Research Journal, 31*(4), 845–862.

- **Foundational: Motivational Frameworks**: **Pintrich, P. R.** (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 452–502). Academic Press.
**Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M.** (1985). *Intrinsic motivation and self-determination in human behavior*. Plenum Press.
**Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L.** (2000). Self-determination theory and the facilitation of intrinsic motivation, social development, and well-being. *American Psychologist, 55*(1), 68–78.
**Dweck, C. S.** (1986). Motivational processes affecting learning. *American Psychologist, 41*(10), 1040–1048.
**Elliot, A. J., & McGregor, H. A.** (2001). A 2×2 achievement goal framework. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 80*(3), 501–519.

- **Foundational: Implementation Intentions and Volition**: **Gollwitzer, P. M.** (1999). Implementation intentions: Strong effects of simple plans. *American Psychologist, 54*(7), 493–503. https://doi.org/10.1037/0003-066X.54.7.493
**Sheeran, P.** (2002). Intention-behavior relations: A conceptual and empirical review. *European Review of Social Psychology, 12*(1), 1–36.
**Milne, S., Orbell, S., & Sheeran, P.** (2002). Combining motivational and volitional interventions to promote exercise participation. *British Journal of Health Psychology, 7*(2), 163–184.

- **Metacognition and Calibration**: **Flavell, J. H.** (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring. *American Psychologist, 34*(10), 906–911.
**Dunlosky, J., & Metcalfe, J.** (2009). *Metacognition*. Sage.
**Hacker, D. J., Dunlosky, J., & Graesser, A. C.** (Eds.). (2009). *Handbook of metacognition in education*. Routledge.

- **Self-Concordance and Long-Horizon Motivation**: **Sheldon, K. M., & Elliot, A. J.** (1999). Goal striving, need satisfaction, and longitudinal well-being. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 76*(3), 482–497.
**Bandura, A.** (1997). *Self-efficacy: The exercise of control*. Freeman.

- **Dweck, C. S. (2006). *Mindset: The New Psychology of Success*. Random House.**: The accessible synthesis of Dweck's three decades of laboratory research, written for a general audience. Introduces the growth/fixed mindset framework to a broad readership and provides extensive case examples across educational, athletic, business, and interpersonal domains. Essential reading for understanding the cultural deployment of mindset theory, though the primary research articles offer more methodological depth.

- **Mueller, C. M., & Dweck, C. S. (1998). Praise for intelligence can undermine children's motivation and performance. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 75*(1), 33–52.**: Six controlled experiments demonstrating that praising children for intelligence (versus effort) after initial success induces more entity-theoretic beliefs, undermines persistence on subsequent difficult tasks, reduces enjoyment, leads to performance-oriented goals, and decreases performance following failure. The methodological centerpiece of the praise research literature and essential reading for anyone interested in the specific feedback implications of mindset theory.

- **Blackwell, L. S., Trzesniewski, K. H., & Dweck, C. S. (2007). Implicit theories of intelligence predict achievement across an adolescent transition. *Child Development, 78*(1), 246–263.**: A two-year longitudinal study of adolescents' math achievement showing that incremental theories predict upward trajectories in grades over the middle school transition — a period notorious for motivational decline. Also reports a mindset intervention study showing that teaching the neuroscience of brain plasticity improved math grades for intervention students. Foundational field evidence linking mindset to real academic outcomes over extended time.

- **Yeager, D. S., Hanselman, P., Walton, G. M., Murray, J. S., Crosnoe, R., Muller, C., … & Dweck, C. S. (2019). A national experiment reveals where a growth mindset improves achievement. *Nature, 573*(7774), 364–369.**: The landmark national RCT involving over 12,000 ninth-grade students across 65 U.S. high schools. Demonstrates that a brief, scalable online mindset intervention improved academic achievement for students performing below the school average, particularly in schools where teachers held high expectations and norms supported challenge-seeking. The most rigorous large-scale test of mindset intervention to date. Essential reading for understanding both the promise and the context-dependence of mindset effects.

- **Sisk, V. F., Burgoyne, A. P., Sun, J., Butler, J. L., & Macnamara, B. N. (2018). To what extent and under which circumstances are growth mindsets important to academic achievement? Two meta-analyses. *Psychological Science, 29*(4), 549–571.**: The most comprehensive meta-analytic synthesis of growth mindset research available at the time of publication, covering 43 intervention studies and the association between mindset and achievement across 273 effect sizes. Finds modest average effects overall, with stronger effects for economically disadvantaged students. Essential for calibrating appropriate confidence in mindset interventions and understanding boundary conditions.

- **Dweck, C. S., & Leggett, E. L. (1988). A social-cognitive approach to motivation and personality. *Psychological Review, 95*(2), 256–273.**: The original theoretical paper articulating the implicit theory → goal orientation → behavioral pattern model that underlies all subsequent mindset research. Presents the theoretical framework with technical precision, distinguishing entity from incremental theories and mapping their implications for motivation and learning. The intellectual foundation of the entire research program.

- **Paunesku, D., Walton, G. M., Romero, C., Smith, E. N., Yeager, D. S., & Dweck, C. S. (2015). Mind-set interventions are a scalable treatment for academic underachievement. *Psychological Science, 26*(6), 784–793.**: Demonstrates that a brief online mindset intervention can be delivered at scale across diverse high schools and produces significant improvements in GPA for at-risk students. Important bridge between laboratory experiments and scalable educational deployment, and one of the first demonstrations of online mindset intervention effectiveness.

- **Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (2000). The "what" and "why" of goal pursuits: Human needs and the self-determination of behavior. *Psychological Inquiry, 11*(4), 227–268.**: Comprehensive statement of Self-Determination Theory, providing the theoretical complement to mindset theory in explaining motivated learning. Articulates the three basic psychological needs (autonomy, competence, relatedness) and their role in sustaining intrinsic motivation. Essential context for understanding how mindset beliefs operate within environmental conditions that support or undermine basic needs.

- **Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L. (2017). *Self-Determination Theory: Basic Psychological Needs in Motivation, Development, and Wellness*. Guilford Press.**: The comprehensive theoretical statement of SDT across domains. Directly relevant to this report's entire motivational architecture analysis (Section 4 and Tier 3 strategies). Chapters on academic motivation and internalization provide the primary empirical and theoretical basis for the regulatory quality framework and basic needs analysis in reading contexts. Recommended reading: Chapters 6 (academic motivation), 8 (internalization), and 14 (self-regulation).

- **Kintsch, W. (1998). *Comprehension: A Paradigm for Cognition*. Cambridge University Press.**: The definitive theoretical statement of the construction-integration model and the three-level text representation framework. Foundational to this report's cognitive architecture analysis (Section 2). Provides the theoretical basis for the distinction between textbase and situation model processing that underlies the entire comprehension-oriented strategy analysis.

- **Zimmerman, B. J., & Schunk, D. H. (Eds.) (2011). *Handbook of Self-Regulation of Learning and Performance*. Routledge.**: Comprehensive reference on self-regulated learning across domains. Relevant to this report's Section 7 and metacognitive self-regulation analysis. Provides extensive empirical grounding for the cyclical SRL model applied to reading and academic work. Key chapters: Zimmerman on self-regulation of learning, Pintrich on goal orientation and self-regulation.

- **Wigfield, A., & Guthrie, J. T. (1997). Relations of children's motivation for reading to the amount and breadth of their reading. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 89*(3), 420–432.**: Seminal empirical study establishing the Motivations for Reading Questionnaire (MRQ) and documenting the multidimensional structure of reading motivation. Foundation for the Section 3 treatment of reading motivation's multidimensional nature. Demonstrated that different motivational dimensions (challenge, curiosity, involvement, importance, recognition, grades, competition, social, compliance, efficacy) predict reading behavior independently.

- **Hidi, S., & Renninger, K. A. (2006). The four-phase model of interest development. *Educational Psychologist, 41*(2), 111–127.**: Primary source for the four-phase interest development model. Directly foundational to the Section 3 treatment of interest theory and the Tier 3 interest-building reading program strategy. Provides the theoretical basis for distinguishing situational from individual interest and specifying the developmental transitions between phases.

- **Bjork, R. A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: Primary source for the desirable difficulties framework. Foundational to the Tier 2 strategy analysis, particularly spaced repetition, retrieval practice, and the cognitive load calibration analysis. Provides the theoretical basis for recommending effortful learning conditions despite their short-term costs.

- **Chi, M. T. H., de Leeuw, N., Chiu, M. H., & LaVancher, C. (1994). Eliciting self-explanations improves understanding. *Cognitive Science, 18*(3), 439–477.**: Seminal empirical demonstration of self-explanation effects on comprehension and learning. Foundational to the Tier 2 self-explanation strategy recommendation. Demonstrated that students who self-explained during learning achieved significantly higher comprehension than those who re-read the same material.

- **Elliot, A. J., & McGregor, H. A. (2001). A 2×2 achievement goal framework. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 80*(3), 501–519.**: Primary source for the 2×2 achievement goal framework. Foundational to this report's Section 5 analysis. Demonstrates the distinct motivational and performance patterns associated with mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance goals.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction, 1*(2), 117–175.**: Primary source and original empirical validation of the reciprocal teaching method. Foundational to this report's Section 7 treatment of social metacognition and collaborative reading. Demonstrated significant comprehension improvements from reciprocal teaching in comparison conditions.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science, 17*(3), 249–255.**: Foundational empirical demonstration of the testing effect (retrieval practice advantage over re-reading). Directly foundational to the Tier 2 retrieval practice strategy recommendation and the calibration training analysis in Section 7.

- **Bjork, R. A., & Bjork, E. L. (1992). A new theory of disuse and the treatment of stimulation as a consequence of conditions during acquisition. In A. Healy, S. Kosslyn, & R. Shiffrin (Eds.), *From learning processes to cognitive processes: Essays in honor of William K. Estes* (Vol. 2, pp. 35–67). Erlbaum.**: The foundational theoretical piece establishing the storage strength vs. retrieval strength distinction that underpins the desirable difficulties framework. Essential for understanding why conditions that reduce immediate performance can enhance long-term retention. Section 2 and 3 of this report rely on this framework.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science, 17*(3), 249–255.**: The landmark experimental demonstration that retrieval practice substantially outperforms re-study over week-long retention intervals. The benchmark empirical study for the testing effect claim made throughout this report. Direct reading recommended for any learner seeking to understand the empirical basis for retrieval practice.

- **Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (2000). The "what" and "why" of goal pursuits: Human needs and the self-determination of behavior. *Psychological Inquiry, 11*(4), 227–268.**: The comprehensive integration of Self-Determination Theory as of 2000, covering the motivational quality continuum, basic psychological needs, and their implications for various life domains including education. The theoretical foundation for Section 4 and 5 of this report.

- **Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed practice in verbal recall tasks: A review and quantitative synthesis. *Psychological Bulletin, 132*(3), 354–380.**: The definitive meta-analytic review of the spacing effect literature as of 2006, synthesizing over 300 studies and establishing effect size estimates and moderating factors. Direct reading recommended for quantitative understanding of the spacing effect magnitude.

- **Chi, M. T. H., Bassok, M., Lewis, M. W., Reimann, P., & Glaser, R. (1989). Self-explanations: How students study and use examples in learning to solve problems. *Cognitive Science, 13*(2), 145–182.**: The original study documenting the self-explanation effect: learners who spontaneously generated self-explanations while studying worked examples showed substantially superior learning and transfer than those who did not. Essential background for Section 3.6.

- **Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2008). Learning concepts and categories: Is spacing the "enemy of induction"? *Psychological Science, 19*(6), 585–592.**: The key study examining interleaving benefits for category learning, demonstrating that interleaved practice produces better induction and transfer than blocked practice despite learners rating blocked practice as more effective. Central to the treatment of the interleaving effect in Section 3.4.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of self-regulation* (pp. 451–502). Academic Press.**: The comprehensive treatment of achievement goals within the self-regulated learning framework. Establishes the empirical and theoretical connections between goal orientation and strategy use that Section 5 builds on. The foundational reference for the SRL architecture employed throughout the latter portion of this report.

- **Barnett, S. M., & Ceci, S. J. (2002). When and where do we apply what we learn? A taxonomy for far transfer. *Psychological Bulletin, 128*(4), 612–637.**: The taxonomic framework for transfer of learning that organized the Far Transfer section. Essential for understanding the dimensions along which near and far transfer vary, and why structural principle extraction is the key mechanism of far transfer.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2002). Becoming a self-regulated learner: An overview. *Theory into Practice, 41*(2), 64–70.**: A concise, accessible overview of the self-regulated learning cycle from one of the framework's principal architects. Provides the forethought-performance-reflection model that Section 5 employs. Recommended as a direct reading complement to the more technical Pintrich 2000 chapter.

- **Pekrun, R., Goetz, T., Titz, W., & Perry, R. P. (2002). Academic emotions in students' self-regulated learning and achievement: A program of qualitative and quantitative research. *Educational Psychologist, 37*(2), 91–105.**: The comprehensive empirical program establishing the role of academic emotions in self-regulated learning and achievement. Foundational for the Control-Value Theory treatment in Section 4.3.

- **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (1990). *A Theory of Goal Setting and Task Performance*. Prentice-Hall.**: The foundational monograph of goal-setting theory. Establishes the four mechanisms (direction, effort, persistence, strategy) and the specificity-difficulty relationship central to this report. Chapter 3 on moderators and Chapter 7 on self-set goals are most relevant.

- **Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a practically useful theory of goal setting and task motivation: A 35-year odyssey. *American Psychologist*, 57(9), 705–717.**: The definitive review summarising 35 years of empirical evidence for goal-setting theory. Includes the most complete statement of the four mediating mechanisms and the boundary conditions for goal effects.

- **Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (1985). *Intrinsic Motivation and Self-Determination in Human Behavior*. Plenum.**: The founding text of self-determination theory. Chapters on cognitive evaluation theory and organismic integration theory are essential background for understanding how goals interact with autonomy, competence, and relatedness needs.

- **Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L. (2000). Self-determination theory and the facilitation of intrinsic motivation, social development, and well-being. *American Psychologist*, 55(1), 68–78.**: The most widely cited overview of SDT, presenting the autonomy-competence-relatedness framework and the internalisation continuum in accessible form. Section on autonomy-supportive environments is directly relevant to PKM design.

- **Elliot, A. J., & McGregor, H. A. (2001). A 2 × 2 achievement goal framework. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 80(3), 501–519.**: The paper introducing the four-cell achievement goal framework that resolves earlier binary models. The mastery-approach cell is the most theoretically relevant to PKM, but the mastery-avoidance cell has underappreciated implications.

- **Mueller, C. M., & Dweck, C. S. (1998). Praise for intelligence can undermine children's motivation and performance. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 74(1), 33–52.**: The landmark study demonstrating that the *linguistic framing* of feedback (process vs. person praise) produces dramatically different motivational trajectories. Foundational evidence for this report's claim that goal language is causally operative.

- **Vansteenkiste, M., Simons, J., Lens, W., Sheldon, K. M., & Deci, E. L. (2004). Motivating learning, performance, and persistence: The synergistic effects of intrinsic goal contents and autonomy-supportive contexts. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*, 87(2), 246–260.**: Demonstrates the interaction between goal contents (intrinsic vs. extrinsic) and context (autonomy-supportive vs. controlling), showing that intrinsic goals in autonomy-supportive contexts produce the strongest learning outcomes.

- **Black, P., & Wiliam, D. (1998). Inside the black box: Raising standards through classroom assessment. *Phi Delta Kappan*, 80(2), 139–148.**: The seminal review establishing that formative assessment produces substantial learning gains. Its framework of transparency, evidence, and gap-closing actions translates directly to PKM assessment-layer design.

- **Zimmerman, B. J. (2000). Attaining self-regulation: A social cognitive perspective. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 13–39). Academic Press.**: The definitive statement of Zimmerman's tripartite SRL model. Essential for understanding how forethought, performance, and self-reflection phases interact and how goal-setting functions as a forethought activity.

- **Pintrich, P. R. (2000). The role of goal orientation in self-regulated learning. In M. Boekaerts, P. R. Pintrich, & M. Zeidner (Eds.), *Handbook of Self-Regulation* (pp. 451–502). Academic Press.**: Integrates achievement goal theory with self-regulated learning theory. The 4×4 framework (phases × areas) introduced here is essential for understanding the full scope of regulatory activity in PKM.

- **Nelson, T. O., & Narens, L. (1990). Metamemory: A theoretical framework and new findings. In G. Bower (Ed.), *The Psychology of Learning and Motivation* (Vol. 26, pp. 125–173). Academic Press.**: The foundational paper on the monitoring-control framework of metacognition. The meta-level/object-level architecture is the basis for understanding how self-assessment operates in learning contexts.

- **Flavell, J. H. (1979). Metacognition and cognitive monitoring: A new area of cognitive-developmental inquiry. *American Psychologist*, 34(10), 906–911.**: The paper that named metacognition as a field and proposed the person-task-strategy taxonomy of metacognitive knowledge. Foundational vocabulary for all subsequent metacognitive research.

- **Ryan, R. M., & Deci, E. L. (2017). *Self-Determination Theory: Basic Psychological Needs in Motivation, Development, and Wellness*. Guilford Press.**: The comprehensive theoretical statement of SDT across domains. Directly relevant to this report's entire motivational architecture analysis (Section 4 and Tier 3 strategies). Chapters on academic motivation and internalization provide the primary empirical and theoretical basis for the regulatory quality framework and basic needs analysis in reading contexts. Recommended reading: Chapters 6 (academic motivation), 8 (internalization), and 14 (self-regulation).

- **Kintsch, W. (1998). *Comprehension: A Paradigm for Cognition*. Cambridge University Press.**: The definitive theoretical statement of the construction-integration model and the three-level text representation framework. Foundational to this report's cognitive architecture analysis (Section 2). Provides the theoretical basis for the distinction between textbase and situation model processing that underlies the entire comprehension-oriented strategy analysis.

- **Zimmerman, B. J., & Schunk, D. H. (Eds.) (2011). *Handbook of Self-Regulation of Learning and Performance*. Routledge.**: Comprehensive reference on self-regulated learning across domains. Relevant to this report's Section 7 and metacognitive self-regulation analysis. Provides extensive empirical grounding for the cyclical SRL model applied to reading and academic work. Key chapters: Zimmerman on self-regulation of learning, Pintrich on goal orientation and self-regulation.

- **Wigfield, A., & Guthrie, J. T. (1997). Relations of children's motivation for reading to the amount and breadth of their reading. *Journal of Educational Psychology, 89*(3), 420–432.**: Seminal empirical study establishing the Motivations for Reading Questionnaire (MRQ) and documenting the multidimensional structure of reading motivation. Foundation for the Section 3 treatment of reading motivation's multidimensional nature. Demonstrated that different motivational dimensions (challenge, curiosity, involvement, importance, recognition, grades, competition, social, compliance, efficacy) predict reading behavior independently.

- **Hidi, S., & Renninger, K. A. (2006). The four-phase model of interest development. *Educational Psychologist, 41*(2), 111–127.**: Primary source for the four-phase interest development model. Directly foundational to the Section 3 treatment of interest theory and the Tier 3 interest-building reading program strategy. Provides the theoretical basis for distinguishing situational from individual interest and specifying the developmental transitions between phases.

- **Bjork, R. A. (1994). Memory and metamemory considerations in the training of human beings. In J. Metcalfe & A. Shimamura (Eds.), *Metacognition: Knowing about knowing* (pp. 185–205). MIT Press.**: Primary source for the desirable difficulties framework. Foundational to the Tier 2 strategy analysis, particularly spaced repetition, retrieval practice, and the cognitive load calibration analysis. Provides the theoretical basis for recommending effortful learning conditions despite their short-term costs.

- **Chi, M. T. H., de Leeuw, N., Chiu, M. H., & LaVancher, C. (1994). Eliciting self-explanations improves understanding. *Cognitive Science, 18*(3), 439–477.**: Seminal empirical demonstration of self-explanation effects on comprehension and learning. Foundational to the Tier 2 self-explanation strategy recommendation. Demonstrated that students who self-explained during learning achieved significantly higher comprehension than those who re-read the same material.

- **Elliot, A. J., & McGregor, H. A. (2001). A 2×2 achievement goal framework. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology, 80*(3), 501–519.**: Primary source for the 2×2 achievement goal framework. Foundational to this report's Section 5 analysis. Demonstrates the distinct motivational and performance patterns associated with mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance goals.

- **Palincsar, A. S., & Brown, A. L. (1984). Reciprocal teaching of comprehension-fostering and comprehension-monitoring activities. *Cognition and Instruction, 1*(2), 117–175.**: Primary source and original empirical validation of the reciprocal teaching method. Foundational to this report's Section 7 treatment of social metacognition and collaborative reading. Demonstrated significant comprehension improvements from reciprocal teaching in comparison conditions.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science, 17*(3), 249–255.**: Foundational empirical demonstration of the testing effect (retrieval practice advantage over re-reading). Directly foundational to the Tier 2 retrieval practice strategy recommendation and the calibration training analysis in Section 7.

- **Bjork, R. A., & Bjork, E. L. (1992). A new theory of disuse and the treatment of stimulation as a consequence of conditions during acquisition. In A. Healy, S. Kosslyn, & R. Shiffrin (Eds.), *From learning processes to cognitive processes: Essays in honor of William K. Estes* (Vol. 2, pp. 35–67). Erlbaum.**: The foundational theoretical piece establishing the storage strength vs. retrieval strength distinction that underpins the desirable difficulties framework. Essential for understanding why conditions that reduce immediate performance can enhance long-term retention. Section 2 and 3 of this report rely on this framework.

- **Roediger, H. L., & Karpicke, J. D. (2006). Test-enhanced learning: Taking memory tests improves long-term retention. *Psychological Science, 17*(3), 249–255.**: The landmark experimental demonstration that retrieval practice substantially outperforms re-study over week-long retention intervals. The benchmark empirical study for the testing effect claim made throughout this report. Direct reading recommended for any learner seeking to understand the empirical basis for retrieval practice.

- **Deci, E. L., & Ryan, R. M. (2000). The "what" and "why" of goal pursuits: Human needs and the self-determination of behavior. *Psychological Inquiry, 11*(4), 227–268.**: The comprehensive integration of Self-Determination Theory as of 2000, covering the motivational quality continuum, basic psychological needs, and their implications for various life domains including education. The theoretical foundation for Section 4 and 5 of this report.

- **Cepeda, N. J., Pashler, H., Vul, E., Wixted, J. T., & Rohrer, D. (2006). Distributed practice in verbal recall tasks: A review and quantitative synthesis. *Psychological Bulletin, 132*(3), 354–380.**: The definitive meta-analytic review of the spacing effect literature as of 2006, synthesizing over 300 studies and establishing effect size estimates and moderating factors. Direct reading recommended for quantitative understanding of the spacing effect magnitude.

- **Chi, M. T. H., Bassok, M., Lewis, M. W., Reimann, P., & Glaser, R. (1989). Self-explanations: How students study and use examples in learning to solve problems. *Cognitive Science, 13*(2), 145–182.**: The original study documenting the self-explanation effect: learners who spontaneously generated self-explanations while studying worked examples showed substantially superior learning and transfer than those who did not. Essential background for Section 3.6.

- **Kornell, N., & Bjork, R. A. (2008). Learning concepts and categories: Is spacing the "enemy of induction"? *Psychological Science, 19*(6), 585–592.**: The key study examining interleaving benefits for category learning, demonstrating that interleaved practice produces better induction and transfer than blocked practice despite learners rating blocked practice as more effective. Central to the treatment of the interleaving effect in Section 3.4.

## Source Attribution

**Extracted from:** [[srl-forethought-self-reflection-pkb-focused-analysis-2026-03-24]]
**Analytical focus:** Deep operational analysis of the Forethought Phase and Self-Reflection Phase of Zimmerman's Cyclical SRL Model, applied specifically to PKB-integrated reading of complex academic texts, with tools, templates, and motivationally-sensitive scaffolding
